View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Petya and Vasya arranged a game. The game runs by the following rules. Players have a directed graph consisting of n vertices and m edges. One of the vertices contains a chip. Initially the chip is located at vertex s. Players take turns moving the chip along some edge of the graph. Petya goes first. Player who can't move the chip loses. If the game lasts for 106 turns the draw is announced.

Vasya was performing big laboratory work in "Spelling and parts of speech" at night before the game, so he fell asleep at the very beginning of the game. Petya decided to take the advantage of this situation and make both Petya's and Vasya's moves.

Your task is to help Petya find out if he can win the game or at least draw a tie.

Input

The first line of input contain two integers n and m — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph (2 ≤ n ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ 2·105).

The next n lines contain the information about edges of the graph. i-th line (1 ≤ i ≤ n) contains nonnegative integer ci — number of vertices such that there is an edge from i to these vertices and ci distinct integers ai, j — indices of these vertices (1 ≤ ai, j ≤ n, ai, j ≠ i).

It is guaranteed that the total sum of ci equals to m.

The next line contains index of vertex s — the initial position of the chip (1 ≤ s ≤ n).

Output

If Petya can win print «Win» in the first line. In the next line print numbers v1, v2, ..., vk (1 ≤ k ≤ 106) — the sequence of vertices Petya should visit for the winning. Vertex v1 should coincide with s. For i = 1... k - 1 there should be an edge from vi to vi + 1 in the graph. There must be no possible move from vertex vk. The sequence should be such that Petya wins the game.

If Petya can't win but can draw a tie, print «Draw» in the only line. Otherwise print «Lose».

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge to adjacent vertex
5. The player who cannot move loses
6. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can:
   - Win (have a sequence that ends with Vasya having no moves)
   - Draw (enter a cycle that continues indefinitely)
   - Lose (no winning or drawing moves available)

## Key Insights

1. This is a game theory problem on a directed graph
2. We need to analyze the game from the end state backward
3. Terminal states: vertices with no outgoing edges
4. A position is winning if there exists a move to a losing position for the opponent
5. A position is losing if all moves lead to winning positions for the opponent
6. We need to detect cycles to determine if a draw is possible

## Approach

1. First, identify all vertices with no outgoing edges (losing positions)
2. Use DFS with memoization to determine winning/losing positions
3. For each vertex, check if there's a path to a terminal state (win for Petya)
4. If we can reach a terminal state in an odd number of moves (Petya's turn), Petya wins
5. If we can enter a cycle, we can achieve a draw
6. If we are forced to go to a winning position for Vasya on every turn, Petya loses

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from input
2. Find all vertices with no outgoing edges (terminal states)
3. Use DFS to determine winning/losing positions
4. Track paths to terminal states for winning
5. Detect cycles for drawing

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            graph[i] = edges
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Find all terminal vertices (no outgoing edges)
    terminal = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal.add(i)
    
    # DFS to determine winning/losing states and find a winning path
    # visited[i] = (state, path) where state is 0 for losing, 1 for winning
    # We'll use memoization to avoid recomputing
    
    # First, find if there's a winning path
    winning_path = []
    
    def dfs(vertex, path):
        # If we've reached a terminal state, we win (since it's not Vasya's turn)
        if vertex in terminal:
            return path + [vertex]
        
        # Try all possible moves
        for next_vertex in graph[vertex]:
            result = dfs(next_vertex, path + [vertex])
            if result:  # If we found a path
                return result
        
        return None
    
    # Try to find a winning path
    winning_path = dfs(s, [])
    
    if winning_path:
        # Check if this path actually wins (Vasya can't move after last move)
        if winning_path[-1] in terminal:
            print("Win")
            print(" ".join(map(str, winning_path)))
            return
    
    # If no winning path, check for draw (cycle)
    # We need to detect if we can enter a cycle
    
    # Use another DFS to detect cycles and find if we can draw
    def has_cycle(start):
        visited = [False] * (n + 1)
        in_stack = [False] * (n + 1)
        path = []
        
        def dfs_cycle(v, path_so_far):
            if v in path:
                # Found cycle - we can draw
                return True
            
            visited[v] = True
            in_stack[v] = True
            path_so_far.append(v)
            
            for neighbor in graph[v]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    if dfs_cycle(neighbor, path_so_far):
                        return True
                elif in_stack[neighbor]:
                    # Found back edge, which means cycle
                    return True
            
            path_so_far.pop()
            in_stack[v] = False
            return False
        
        # Start from the initial vertex
        if dfs_cycle(start, []):
            return True
        
        return False
    
    # Check if there's a cycle we can enter
    if has_cycle(s):
        print("Draw")
        return
    
    # If we can't win and can't draw, we lose
    print("Lose")

solve()
```

Actually, let me think more systematically:

3. A draw occurs when we can enter a cycle

Since we need to check if Petya can win, then draw, or lose:

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            graph[i] = edges
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Find all terminal vertices (no outgoing edges)
    terminal = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal.add(i)
    
    # DFS to check if there's a winning path and find it
    def find_winning_path():
        # We need to find a path that ends at a terminal vertex
        # and the length of the path is odd (since Petya goes first)
        
        stack = [(s, [s])]
        
        while stack:
            current, path = stack.pop()
            
            # If current is terminal, we found a winning position
            if current in terminal:
                return path
            
            # Explore all outgoing edges
            for next_vertex in graph[current]:
                stack.append((next_vertex, path + [next_vertex]))
        
        return None
    
    winning_path = find_winning_path()
    
    if winning_path:
        print("Win")
        print(" ".join(map(str, winning_path)))
        return
    
    # If no winning path, check for draw (cycle)
    # We need to check if we can enter a cycle from the starting position
    # A cycle means we can go on forever, resulting in a draw
    
    # Use cycle detection
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    path = []
    
    def has_cycle(v):
        if visited[v]:
            return False
            
        visited[v] = True
        path.append(v)
        
        for w in graph[v]:
            if w == path[0]:  # Found a cycle from start
                return True
            if has_cycle(w):
                return True
                
        path.pop()
        return False
    
    # Actually, we need to check if there's any cycle in the reachable component
    # Better approach: use DFS to detect if we can reach a cycle
    
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    path = []
    
    def dfs_detect_cycle(v):
        if v in path:
            return True
            
        visited[v] = True
        path.append(v)
        
        for next_v in graph[v]:
            if dfs_detect_cycle(next_v):
                return True
                
        path.pop()
        return False
    
    # Check if we can reach a cycle from start
    can_draw = False
    temp_path = []
    
    def dfs_with_path(v, path_so_far):
        nonlocal can_draw
        
        if v in path_so_far:
            can_draw = True
            return
            
        if v in temp_path:
            return
            
        temp_path.append(v)
        path_so_far.append(v)
        
        for next_v in graph[v]:
            dfs_with_path(next_v, path_so_far)
            
        path_so_far.pop()
        temp_path.pop()
    
    # Start DFS from s
    dfs_with_path(s, [])
    
    if can_draw:
        print("Draw")
        return
    
    print("Lose")

solve()
```

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build adjacency list
graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]

for i in range(1, n + 1):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    c = line[0]
    if c > 0:
        edges = line[1:]
        graph[i] = edges

s = int(input())

# Find all terminal vertices (no outgoing edges)
terminal = set()
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    if len(graph[i]) == 0:
        terminal.add(i)

# Find a winning path using BFS (to ensure we find the shortest path)
from collections import deque

def find_winning_path():
    # We want to find a path that ends at a terminal vertex
    # We'll use BFS to find the shortest path
    
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited = set()
    visited.add(s)
    
    while queue:
        current, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # If current is terminal, we have a winning path
        if current in terminal:
            return path
        
        # Explore all outgoing edges
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            next_path = path + [next_vertex]
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, next_path))
    
    return None

winning_path = find_winning_path()

if winning_path:
    print("Win")
    print(" ".join(map(str, winning_path)))
else:
    # Check for cycle - if we can reach a vertex that we've seen before in the same path
    # or if we can enter a cycle of length > 1
    
    # Use DFS with path tracking
    path = []
    visited = set()
    
    def has_cycle(v, path_so_far):
        if v in path_so_far:
            # Found cycle - we can draw
            return True
            
        path_so_far.append(v)
        
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if has_cycle(neighbor, path_so_far):
                return True
                
        path_so_far.pop()
        return False
    
    # Check if any path from s leads to a cycle
    cycle_found = has_cycle(s, [])
    
    if cycle_found:
        print("Draw")
    else:
        print("Lose")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge (from current vertex to a neighbor)
5. The player who cannot move loses
6. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

This is essentially a game theory problem on a directed graph. The outcome depends on:
- Whether Petya can force a win (by making the game end on Vasya's turn)
- Whether Petya can force a draw (by entering a cycle)

The game ends when a player cannot move from the current position.

## Analysis

1. **Win condition**: Petya wins if he can move to a position where Vasya has no valid moves (i.e., a dead end). This means we need to find a path that ends at a vertex with no outgoing edges.

2. **Draw condition**: Petya can draw if he can enter a cycle (repeating sequence of vertices). Since the game has a maximum of 10^6 turns, if Petya can force a cycle, it will result in a draw.

3. **Lose condition**: If all possible paths eventually lead to Vasya winning, then Petya loses.

## Strategy

1. First, identify all vertices with no outgoing edges (dead ends). These are winning positions for the player who moves to them.

2. Use DFS/BFS to explore the game tree and determine the outcome from each vertex:
   - If a vertex leads to a dead end on the next move, that's a win for the current player (who makes that move)
   - If a vertex leads to cycles, that's a draw

3. We need to detect if there's a path that ends at a dead end (win) or if there's a cycle (draw).

## Implementation Approach

1. Build the graph from input
2. Find vertices with no outgoing edges (dead ends)
3. Use DFS to trace paths from start vertex to dead ends
4. If we find a path to a dead end, return "Win" with that path
5. If we find a cycle (path that repeats), return "Draw"
6. Otherwise, return "Lose"

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            graph[i] = edges
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Find vertices with no outgoing edges (dead ends)
    dead_ends = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            dead_ends.add(i)
    
    # If start position is already a dead end, Petya can't move and loses immediately
    if s in dead_ends:
        print("Lose")
        return
    
    # Try to find a winning path (path that ends at a dead end)
    # We'll do BFS to find the shortest path to a dead end
    
    # Keep track of visited vertices in current path to detect cycles
    visited_in_path = set()
    path = []
    
    # We'll use BFS to find all paths to dead ends
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited_vertices = set()
    
    while queue:
        current, current_path = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we've already visited this vertex in current path (cycle detected)
        if current in visited_in_path:
            # We found a cycle - this will lead to draw
            print("Draw")
            return
        
        # If current vertex is a dead end, we found a winning path
        if current in dead_ends:
            print("Win")
            print(' '.join(map(str, current_path)))
            return
        
        # Add current vertex to visited in path
        visited_in_path.add(current)
        
        # Explore all neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            # Only explore if we haven't visited this neighbor in current path
            if neighbor not in visited_in_path:
                new_path = current_path + [neighbor]
                queue.append((neighbor, new_path))
        
        # Remove current vertex from visited_in_path after processing
        # (for next iterations, we want to explore different paths)
        # Actually, we need to reconsider - we should not remove it immediately
        # Instead, we'll just keep track of the path for each node
        
    # If we get here, no winning path was found
    # Check if there's a cycle that can be formed
    
    # Let's try to detect cycles using different approach
    # We'll search for cycles in the graph starting from s
    
    # Use DFS with path tracking
    path_stack = []
    visited = set()
    
    def dfs(node, path):
        if node in path:
            # Found a cycle
            cycle_start = path.index(node)
            cycle = path[cycle_start:]
            print("Draw")
            return True
        
        if node in dead_ends:
            # Found a dead end - winning path found
            print("Win")
            print(' '.join(map(str, path + [node])))
            return True
        
        # Mark current node as visited in current DFS path
        path.append(node)
        visited.add(node)
        
        # Explore all neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                if dfs(neighbor, path):
                    return True
        
        # Backtrack
        path.pop()
        return False
    
    # Try DFS to find either a winning path or a cycle
    path = []
    if dfs(s, path):
        return
    
    # If no winning path and no cycle found, Petya loses
    print("Lose")

solve()
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            graph[i] = edges
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Find vertices with no outgoing edges (dead ends)
    dead_ends = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            dead_ends.add(i)
    
    # If start position is already a dead end, Petya can't move and loses
    if s in dead_ends:
        print("Lose")
        return
    
    # Try to find a winning path (path that ends at a dead end)
    # Use BFS to find shortest path to any dead end
    
    # We need to track the path to avoid cycles
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited = set()
    
    while queue:
        current, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we've already seen this vertex in current path, we have a cycle
        # But we need to check if we've already processed this state
        # Instead, let's just stop if we find a dead end
        
        # If current is a dead end, we found a winning path
        if current in dead_ends:
            print("Win")
            print(' '.join(map(str, path)))
            return
        
        # Explore neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            # Only add if we haven't visited this path state
            # But we need to prevent infinite loops
            new_path = path + [neighbor]
            queue.append((neighbor, new_path))
    
    # If no winning path found, check if there's a cycle
    # We'll use DFS to detect cycles
    
    def has_cycle_and_draw(start):
        visited = set()
        path = []
        
        def dfs(node):
            if node in path:
                return True  # cycle found
            
            if node in visited:
                return False
            
            visited.add(node)
            path.append(node)
            
            for neighbor in graph[node]:
                if dfs(neighbor):
                    return True
            
            path.pop()
            return False
        
        return dfs(start)
    
    if has_cycle_and_draw(s):
        print("Draw")
    else:
        print("Lose")

solve()
```

Actually, the cycle detection might not be reliable. Let me think again.

The key insight is that if we can reach a cycle, then we can keep playing indefinitely, resulting in a draw. But we also need to check if we can win by reaching a dead end.

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            graph[i] = edges
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Find vertices with no outgoing edges (dead ends)
    dead_ends = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            dead_ends.add(i)
    
    # If start position is a dead end, Petya can't move and loses
    if s in dead_ends:
        print("Lose")
        return
    
    # First, try to find a winning path to a dead end
    # Use BFS since we want the shortest path
    
    from collections import deque
    
    # State: (current_vertex, path)
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited_states = set()
    
    while queue:
        current, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # If current vertex is a dead end, we found a winning path
        if current in dead_ends:
            print("Win")
            print(' '.join(map(str, path)))
            return
        
        # If we've seen this state before (cycle detected), it's a draw
        state = (current, tuple(path))
        if state in visited_states:
            # Found a cycle - this leads to draw
            print("Draw")
            return
        
        visited_states.add(state)
        
        # Explore all neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            new_path = path + [neighbor]
            queue.append((neighbor, new_path))
    
    # If we get here, no winning path and no cycle was found
    print("Lose")

solve()
```

Wait, this has a flaw - we're not detecting cycles properly in the BFS. We need to detect if we can reach a cycle from the start.

Let me revise with a cleaner approach:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build adjacency list
graph = defaultdict(list)
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    c = line[0]
    if c > 0:
        edges = line[1:]
        graph[i] = edges

s = int(input())

# Find vertices with no outgoing edges (dead ends)
dead_ends = set()
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    if len(graph[i]) == 0:
        dead_ends.add(i)

# If start position is already a dead end, Petya can't move and loses
if s in dead_ends:
    print("Lose")
else:
    # BFS to find path to dead end or detect cycle
    from collections import deque
    
    # Each state is (vertex, path)
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited_states = set()
    
    found_win = False
    found_cycle = False
    
    while queue:
        current, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if current vertex is a dead end
        if current in dead_ends:
            print("Win")
            print(' '.join(map(str, path)))

            break

        state = (current, tuple(path))
        if state in visited_states:
            found_cycle = True
            break
        
        visited_states.add(state)
        
        # Explore next vertices
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            new_path = path + [next_vertex]
            queue.append((next_vertex, new_path))
    
    if found_win:
        pass
    elif found_cycle:
        print("Draw")
    else:
        print("Lose")
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Petya and Vasya arranged a game. The game runs by the following rules. Players have a directed graph consisting of n vertices and m edges. One of the vertices contains a chip. Initially the chip is located at vertex s. Players take turns moving the chip along some edge of the graph. Petya goes first. Player who can't move the chip loses. If the game lasts for 106 turns the draw is announced.

Vasya was performing big laboratory work in "Spelling and parts of speech" at night before the game, so he fell asleep at the very beginning of the game. Petya decided to take the advantage of this situation and make both Petya's and Vasya's moves.

Your task is to help Petya find out if he can win the game or at least draw a tie.

Input

The first line of input contain two integers n and m — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph (2 ≤ n ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ 2·105).

The next n lines contain the information about edges of the graph. i-th line (1 ≤ i ≤ n) contains nonnegative integer ci — number of vertices such that there is an edge from i to these vertices and ci distinct integers ai, j — indices of these vertices (1 ≤ ai, j ≤ n, ai, j ≠ i).

It is guaranteed that the total sum of ci equals to m.

The next line contains index of vertex s — the initial position of the chip (1 ≤ s ≤ n).

Output

If Petya can win print «Win» in the first line. In the next line print numbers v1, v2, ..., vk (1 ≤ k ≤ 106) — the sequence of vertices Petya should visit for the winning. Vertex v1 should coincide with s. For i = 1... k - 1 there should be an edge from vi to vi + 1 in the graph. There must be no possible move from vertex vk. The sequence should be such that Petya wins the game.

If Petya can't win but can draw a tie, print «Draw» in the only line. Otherwise print «Lose».

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge to an adjacent vertex
5. The player who can't move loses
6. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

This is essentially a game theory problem where we need to determine the winning/losing positions.

1. A position is "losing" if all moves from that position lead to a winning position for the opponent
2. A position is "winning" if there exists at least one move that leads to a losing position for the opponent
3. A position with no outgoing edges is losing (the player whose turn it is loses)

However, since Petya can make both his and Vasya's moves (he's controlling the flow), we need to think about this differently.

Actually, reading the problem again: "Petya decided to take the advantage of this situation and make both Petya's and Vasya's moves." This means Petya controls the entire sequence - he chooses all moves.

So Petya wants to:
- Win: reach a position where Vasya has no moves (and it's Vasya's turn)
- Draw: enter a cycle that never ends (after 10^6 moves)

## Strategy

1. First, check if there's a path from the starting position to any sink (node with no outgoing edges). If Petya can reach such a node, he wins.

2. If no such path exists, check if there's a cycle that Petya can enter and potentially stay in indefinitely (drawing a tie).

3. If Petya cannot reach any sink and cannot enter a cycle, he loses.

## Algorithm

1. Use DFS/BFS to find all reachable nodes from the starting position
2. For each reachable node, determine if it's part of a winning path or a cycle
3. Check if there's a path to a sink (node with no outgoing edges)
4. If such path exists, return the winning path
5. If no path to sink exists, check if there's a cycle reachable from start
6. If cycle exists, return "Draw"
7. Otherwise, return "Lose"

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            vertices = line[1:c+1]
            graph[i] = vertices
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Check if there's a path to a sink (node with no outgoing edges)
    # First, find all nodes that have no outgoing edges
    sinks = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            sinks.add(i)
    
    # BFS to find path to sink (where we reach a node with no outgoing edges)
    # and track the path
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited = {s}
    
    while queue:
        node, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # If this node is a sink, Petya can win by reaching here
        if node in sinks:
            print("Win")
            print(" ".join(map(str, path)))
            return
        
        # Explore neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                visited.add(neighbor)
                new_path = path + [neighbor]
                queue.append((neighbor, new_path))
    
    # If we reach here, no path to sink exists
    # Check if there's a cycle (we need to detect cycles in reachable components)
    
    # Use DFS to detect cycles and build the path
    visited = set()
    rec_stack = set()
    
    def dfs_cycle_detection(u, path):
        visited.add(u)
        rec_stack.add(u)
        
        for v in graph[u]:
            if v not in visited:
                if dfs_cycle_detection(v, path + [v]):
                    return True
            elif v in rec_stack:
                # Found a cycle
                return True
        
        rec_stack.remove(u)
        return False
    
    # Find if any reachable node is part of a cycle
    # But we need to find if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    
    # Let's do a BFS from start to find all reachable nodes
    reachable = set()
    queue = deque([s])
    reachable.add(s)
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor not in reachable:
                reachable.add(neighbor)
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Check if any of the reachable nodes are part of a cycle
    # We'll do DFS on reachable nodes to find cycles
    visited = set()
    rec_stack = set()
    
    def has_cycle_from(start, reachable):
        if start in visited:
            return False
        
        visited.add(start)
        rec_stack.add(start)
        
        for neighbor in graph[start]:
            if neighbor in rec_stack:
                # Found a cycle
                return True
            if neighbor in reachable and neighbor not in visited:
                if has_cycle_from(neighbor, reachable):
                    return True
        
        rec_stack.remove(start)
        return False
    
    # Check if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    for node in reachable:
        if has_cycle_from(node, reachable):
            # Found a cycle, so we can draw
            print("Draw")
            return
    
    # If no path to sink and no cycle, Petya loses
    print("Lose")

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):

            graph[i] = vertices

    # Find all nodes with no outgoing edges (sinks)
    sinks = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            sinks.add(i)
    
    # BFS to find path to sink
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited = {s}
    
    while queue:
        node, path = queue.popleft()

        if node in sinks:

    def dfs_has_cycle(node):
        if node in visited:
            return False
            
        visited.add(node)
        rec_stack.add(node)
        
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor in rec_stack:
                # Found a cycle
                return True
            if neighbor in reachable and not dfs_has_cycle(neighbor):
                continue
        
        rec_stack.remove(node)
        return False
    
    # Try to find any cycle starting from reachable nodes
    for node in reachable:
        if dfs_has_cycle(node):
            print("Draw")
            return
    
    # If no path to sink and no cycle, Petya loses
    print("Lose")
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build adjacency list
graph = defaultdict(list)
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    c = line[0]
    if c > 0:
        vertices = line[1:c+1]
        graph[i] = vertices

s = int(input())

# Find all sinks (nodes with no outgoing edges)
sinks = set()
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    if len(graph[i]) == 0:
        sinks.add(i)

# BFS to find path to sink
queue = deque([(s, [s])])
visited = {s}

while queue:
    node, path = queue.popleft()
    
    # If we reached a sink, Petya wins
    if node in sinks:
        print("Win")
        print(" ".join(map(str, path)))
        exit()
    
    # Explore neighbors
    for neighbor in graph[node]:
        if neighbor not in visited:
            visited.add(neighbor)

    for neighbor in graph[node]:
        if neighbor not in reachable:
            reachable.add(neighbor)
            queue.append(neighbor)

# Use DFS with recursion stack

rec_stack = set()

def has_cycle(u):
    if u in visited:
        return False
    
    visited.add(u)
    rec_stack.add(u)
    
    for v in graph[u]:
        if v in rec_stack:
            return True
        if v in reachable and has_cycle(v):
            return True
    
    rec_stack.remove(u)
    return False

    if has_cycle(node):
        cycle_found = True
        break
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Players take turns moving the chip along edges, Petya goes first
4. The player who can't move loses (win for the other player)
5. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
6. Petya can make both his own moves and Vasya's moves (he controls the entire game)

We need to determine if Petya can:
- Win (find a winning path)
- Draw (find a cycle to make the game last indefinitely)
- Lose (no winning or drawing strategy)

## Key Insights

1. Since Petya controls both moves, he can force the game to go through any sequence of vertices
2. If there's a path from start to a vertex with no outgoing edges, Petya can win by following that path
3. If there's a cycle that Petya can enter and stay in (by moving to the cycle and then moving between vertices in the cycle), the game can continue forever, resulting in a draw
4. If Petya can't reach any terminal vertex (vertex with no outgoing edges) and there's no cycle, he loses

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from input
2. Identify all vertices with no outgoing edges (terminal vertices)
3. Check if there's a path from the start vertex to any terminal vertex. If yes, Petya can win.
4. If no winning path exists, check if there's a cycle reachable from the start vertex. If yes, Petya can draw by staying in the cycle.
5. Otherwise, Petya loses.

For cycles detection: we can use DFS with state tracking to detect cycles, or we can use the concept of strongly connected components.

Actually, let me think more carefully:

In this game, since Petya controls both moves, he can:
- Win if he can reach a vertex with no outgoing edges (terminal vertex)
- Draw if he can reach a cycle (and then stay in it forever)
- Lose if neither of the above is possible

So the algorithm:
1. Build adjacency list
2. Find all vertices with no outgoing edges (terminal vertices)
3. Do BFS/DFS from start vertex to see if we can reach any terminal vertex
4. If yes, return the path to that terminal vertex
5. If no, check if there's any cycle reachable from start vertex
6. If cycle exists, return "Draw"
7. Otherwise, return "Lose"

For cycle detection from a start vertex, we can use DFS with visited states:
- Visited: 0 = unvisited, 1 = visiting, 2 = visited
- If we visit a vertex that's currently being visited, we have a cycle

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            for edge in edges:
                graph[i].append(edge)
    
    start = int(input())
    
    # Find terminal vertices (vertices with no outgoing edges)
    terminal_vertices = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal_vertices.add(i)
    
    # Check if we can reach any terminal vertex from start
    # Use BFS to find path to any terminal vertex
    visited = set()
    queue = deque([(start, [start])])
    visited.add(start)
    
    # First, try to find a winning path
    winning_path = None
    
    while queue:
        curr, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # If current vertex is terminal, we found a winning path
        if curr in terminal_vertices:
            winning_path = path
            break
            
        # Explore neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[curr]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                visited.add(neighbor)
                queue.append((neighbor, path + [neighbor]))
    
    if winning_path:
        print("Win")
        print(*winning_path)
        return
    
    # If no winning path, check if there's a cycle we can enter
    # We'll use DFS to detect cycles from start
    visited = [0] * (n + 1)  # 0: unvisited, 1: visiting, 2: visited
    path = []
    cycle_detected = False
    cycle_path = []
    
    def dfs(u, path):
        nonlocal cycle_detected, cycle_path
        
        # Mark current vertex as visiting
        visited[u] = 1
        path.append(u)
        
        # Check neighbors
        for v in graph[u]:
            if visited[v] == 0:
                dfs(v, path)
            elif visited[v] == 1:
                # Found a cycle - we can stay in it forever
                cycle_detected = True
                cycle_path = path[:]
                # We can terminate here since we found a cycle
                return
        
        # Backtrack
        path.pop()
        visited[u] = 2
    
    # We need to check if any path from start leads to a cycle
    # But we can also look for cycles reachable from start
    visited = [0] * (n + 1)
    path = []
    
    # Use DFS from start to detect cycles
    def has_cycle_with_path(u, path):
        visited[u] = 1
        path.append(u)
        
        for v in graph[u]:
            if visited[v] == 0:
                if has_cycle_with_path(v, path):
                    return True
            elif visited[v] == 1:
                # Found a cycle
                return True
                
        path.pop()
        visited[u] = 2
        return False
    
    # Try to see if we have a cycle reachable from start
    visited = [0] * (n + 1)
    path = []
    
    if has_cycle_with_path(start, path):
        print("Draw")
        # We need to output a path that goes to the cycle and stays in it
        # Actually, we just need to show a draw is possible
        # We can output a path that goes to the cycle (and then repeats)
        # But we just need to say "Draw"
        return
    
    # If no winning path and no cycle, then lose
    print("Lose")

solve()
```

Let me simplify and fix:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):

                graph[i].append(edge)
    
    start = int(input())

    terminal_vertices = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):

            terminal_vertices.add(i)
    
    # Check if we can reach any terminal vertex from start using BFS
    visited = set()

    visited.add(start)
    
    winning_path = None
    
    while queue:
        curr, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # If current vertex is terminal, we found a winning path
        if curr in terminal_vertices:
            winning_path = path
            break

        for neighbor in graph[curr]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                visited.add(neighbor)
                queue.append((neighbor, path + [neighbor]))
    
    if winning_path:
        print("Win")
        print(*winning_path)
        return
    
    # If no winning path, check if there's a cycle reachable from start
    # We'll use DFS with recursion stack to detect cycles
    visited = [0] * (n + 1)  # 0: unvisited, 1: visiting, 2: finished
    path = []
    
    def has_cycle(u):
        if visited[u] == 2:
            return False
            
        if visited[u] == 1:
            return True  # Found a cycle
            
        visited[u] = 1
        path.append(u)
        
        for v in graph[u]:
            if has_cycle(v):
                return True
                
        path.pop()
        visited[u] = 2
        return False
    
    # Check if there's a cycle reachable from start
    if has_cycle(start):
        print("Draw")
        return
    
    # No winning path and no cycle, so Petya loses
    print("Lose")
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Petya and Vasya arranged a game. The game runs by the following rules. Players have a directed graph consisting of n vertices and m edges. One of the vertices contains a chip. Initially the chip is located at vertex s. Players take turns moving the chip along some edge of the graph. Petya goes first. Player who can't move the chip loses. If the game lasts for 106 turns the draw is announced.

Vasya was performing big laboratory work in "Spelling and parts of speech" at night before the game, so he fell asleep at the very beginning of the game. Petya decided to take the advantage of this situation and make both Petya's and Vasya's moves.

Your task is to help Petya find out if he can win the game or at least draw a tie.

Input

The first line of input contain two integers n and m — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph (2 ≤ n ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ 2·105).

The next n lines contain the information about edges of the graph. i-th line (1 ≤ i ≤ n) contains nonnegative integer ci — number of vertices such that there is an edge from i to these vertices and ci distinct integers ai, j — indices of these vertices (1 ≤ ai, j ≤ n, ai, j ≠ i).

It is guaranteed that the total sum of ci equals to m.

The next line contains index of vertex s — the initial position of the chip (1 ≤ s ≤ n).

Output

If Petya can win print «Win» in the first line. In the next line print numbers v1, v2, ..., vk (1 ≤ k ≤ 106) — the sequence of vertices Petya should visit for the winning. Vertex v1 should coincide with s. For i = 1... k - 1 there should be an edge from vi to vi + 1 in the graph. There must be no possible move from vertex vk. The sequence should be such that Petya wins the game.

If Petya can't win but can draw a tie, print «Draw» in the only line. Otherwise print «Lose».

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge from current vertex
5. The player who cannot move loses
6. If game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

This is a combinatorial game theory problem. Let me think about the game states:

1. A vertex is a "winning position" if the current player can force a win
2. A vertex is a "losing position" if no matter what the current player does, the opponent can force a win
3. A vertex is a "draw position" if the game can continue indefinitely (cycle)

For a position to be winning for the current player:
- There exists at least one move to a position that is losing for the opponent

For a position to be losing for the current player:
- Every possible move leads to a position that is winning for the opponent

For a position to be a draw:
- There exists a cycle that can be traversed indefinitely

## Algorithm

1. First, check if there's a cycle reachable from the starting position
2. If there's a cycle, Petya can draw by entering and cycling in that cycle
3. If no cycle exists, we need to determine if Petya can force a win

To check if there's a cycle:
- Use DFS to detect cycles from the starting position
- If we find a cycle, return "Draw"

To determine if Petya can win:
- Use dynamic programming with memoization to determine winning/losing positions
- Start from positions with no outgoing edges (losing for current player)
- Work backwards

However, the constraint that the game ends in 10^6 moves means we need to detect infinite games through cycles.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Build the graph from input
2. Use DFS to detect cycles from the starting vertex
3. If cycle detected, return "Draw"
4. If no cycle, use game theory to determine if starting position is winning

So my approach:
1. Detect if there's a cycle reachable from start

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]

            edges = line[1:]

        visited[v] = True
        rec_stack[v] = True

            if not visited[neighbor]:
                if has_cycle(neighbor, visited, rec_stack):
                    return True
            elif rec_stack[neighbor]:
                return True

    # A vertex is losing if all its neighbors are winning
```

Wait, I see an issue. In a finite directed acyclic graph, there must be at least one sink (no outgoing edges) because otherwise we could keep moving forever. But since we already detected cycles, in our acyclic case, there must be some vertices with no outgoing edges.

Let me fix the code to handle edge cases properly.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]

for i in range(1, n + 1):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    c = line[0]
    if c > 0:
        edges = line[1:]
        graph[i] = edges

# Check for cycles using DFS
def has_cycle(v, visited, rec_stack):
    visited[v] = True
    rec_stack[v] = True
    
    for neighbor in graph[v]:
        if not visited[neighbor]:
            if has_cycle(neighbor, visited, rec_stack):
                return True
        elif rec_stack[neighbor]:
            return True
    
    rec_stack[v] = False
    return False

visited = [False] * (n + 1)
rec_stack = [False] * (n + 1)

if has_cycle_from_s:
    print("Draw")

    # If no cycle, determine if Petya wins with game theory
    # A position is losing if all moves go to winning positions
    # A position is winning if there exists a move to a losing position
    
    # First, all vertices with no outgoing edges are losing positions
    # because the current player cannot move and loses
    
    losing = [False] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            losing[i] = True
    
    # Iterate until stable - this should converge in a finite number of steps
    # Each iteration we update positions that can reach a losing position
    changed = True
    while changed:
        changed = False
        for u in range(1, n + 1):
            # Check if we can move from u to a losing position
            can_move_to_losing = False
            for v in graph[u]:
                if losing[v]:
                    can_move_to_losing = True
                    break
            
            # If we can move to a losing position, then u is winning
            # But we're tracking losing positions - if u is winning, we don't mark it as losing
            if not losing[u] and can_move_to_losing:
                losing[u] = True
                changed = True
    
    # If Petya's starting position is losing, he loses
    if losing[s]:
        print("Lose")
    else:
        print("Win")
        # Find a path from s to any losing position
        # We want to reach a vertex that is losing
        
        # Find any path from s to a vertex with no outgoing edges
        # Since we have no cycles, such a path exists
        
        from collections import deque
        
        queue = deque([(s, [s])])
        visited = set([s])
        
        path_found = None
        while queue:
            curr, path = queue.popleft()
            
            # If current vertex has no outgoing edges, it's a losing position
            if len(graph[curr]) == 0:
                path_found = path
                break
            
            for next_node in graph[curr]:
                if next_node not in visited:
                    visited.add(next_node)
                    queue.append((next_node, path + [next_node]))
        
        print(*path_found)
```

    c = line[0]
    if c > 0:
        edges = line[1:]
        graph[i] = edges

s = int(input())

# Check for cycles using DFS
def has_cycle(v, visited, rec_stack):
    visited[v] = True
    rec_stack[v] = True
    
    for neighbor in graph[v]:
        if not visited[neighbor]:
            if has_cycle(neighbor, visited, rec_stack):
                return True
        elif rec_stack[neighbor]:
            return True
    
    rec_stack[v] = False
    return False

visited = [False] * (n + 1)
rec_stack = [False] * (n + 1)

has_cycle_from_s = has_cycle(s, visited, rec_stack)

if has_cycle_from_s:
    print("Draw")
else:
    # If no cycle, determine if Petya wins with game theory
    # A position is losing if all moves go to winning positions
    # A position is winning if there exists a move to a losing position
    
    # First, all vertices with no outgoing edges are losing positions
    # because the current player cannot move and loses
    
    losing = [False] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            losing[i] = True
    
    # Iterate until stable - this should converge in a finite number of steps
    # Each iteration we update positions that can reach a losing position
    changed = True
    while changed:
        changed = False
        for u in range(1, n + 1):
            # Check if we can move from u to a losing position
            can_move_to_losing = False
            for v in graph[u]:
                if losing[v]:
                    can_move_to_losing = True
                    break
            
            # If we can move to a losing position, then u is winning
            # But we're tracking losing positions - if u is winning, we don't mark it as losing
            if not losing[u] and can_move_to_losing:
                losing[u] = True
                changed = True
    
    # If Petya's starting position is losing, he loses
    if losing[s]:
        print("Lose")
    else:
        print("Win")
        # Find a path from s to any losing position
        # We want to reach a vertex that is losing
        
        # Find any path from s to a vertex with no outgoing edges
        # Since we have no cycles, such a path exists
        
        from collections import deque
        
        queue = deque([(s, [s])])
        visited = set([s])
        
        path_found = None
        while queue:
            curr, path = queue.popleft()
            
            # If current vertex has no outgoing edges, it's a losing position
            if len(graph[curr]) == 0:
                path_found = path
                break
            
            for next_node in graph[curr]:
                if next_node not in visited:
                    visited.add(next_node)
                    queue.append((next_node, path + [next_node]))
        
        print(*path_found)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge to a neighboring vertex
5. The player who can't move loses
6. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

1. This is a game theory problem on a directed graph
2. We need to analyze the winning/losing positions
3. A position is a losing position if all moves from it lead to winning positions for the opponent
4. A position is a winning position if there exists at least one move to a losing position for the opponent
5. We can use dynamic programming with memoization to determine win/lose states
6. If we can find a path that ends at a terminal vertex (no outgoing edges), Petya wins
7. If we can find a cycle that Petya can traverse indefinitely, it's a draw
8. If all paths eventually lead to a position where Petya cannot move, Petya loses

## Algorithm

1. Build the graph from input
2. Use DFS with cycle detection to find if there's a winning path to an end state
3. If we can reach a vertex with no outgoing edges, Petya wins
4. If we can find a cycle that Petya can traverse, it's a draw
5. Otherwise, Petya loses

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            vertices = line[1:c+1]
            graph[i] = vertices
    
    start = int(input())
    
    # Check if we can win by finding a path to a terminal vertex
    # Use DFS with path tracking to find winning path
    visited = set()
    path = []
    
    def dfs(current):
        nonlocal path
        if current in visited:
            # We found a cycle, check if we can create a draw
            return "draw"
        
        visited.add(current)
        
        # Check if current vertex has no outgoing edges
        if len(graph[current]) == 0:
            # Petya wins by reaching a terminal vertex
            if path:
                path.append(current)
            return "win"
        
        # Try each possible move
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            # If we can reach a win from this next state, we can win from current
            result = dfs(next_vertex)
            if result == "win":
                path.append(current)
                path.append(next_vertex)
                return "win"
        
        # If we can't win from any move, we might be in a losing position
        # But we need to check if we can draw via cycle
        # Remove current from visited to check cycles
        visited.remove(current)
        return "lose"
    
    # First, try to find a winning path
    path.clear()
    visited.clear()
    
    # We need to be careful about cycle detection
    # Let's use a different approach: find if there's a path to a terminal vertex
    # and if there's a cycle that can be used for draw
    
    # First, check if we can reach a terminal vertex
    if dfs(start) == "win":
        # We found a winning path
        print("Win")
        print(" ".join(map(str, path)))
        return
    
    # If we can't win, check if we can draw (find a cycle)
    # We need to detect cycles in the game graph
    # A cycle allows for infinite play, which is a draw
    
    # Use DFS to detect cycles
    visited = set()
    stack = []
    
    def has_cycle(v, parent):
        visited.add(v)
        stack.append(v)
        
        for next_v in graph[v]:
            if next_v not in visited:
                if has_cycle(next_v, v):
                    return True
            elif next_v != parent:
                # Found a cycle
                return True
        
        stack.pop()
        return False
    
    # Check if there's any cycle in the graph
    # Actually, we need to check if there's a cycle that we can use to create a draw
    # We can create a draw if we can find any cycle that the chip can traverse
    
    # Better approach: since we can't win, but we can draw if there's a cycle
    # We need to check if the chip can be trapped in a cycle
    
    has_cycle_found = False
    visited_cycle = set()
    
    def detect_cycle(start_v):
        stack = []
        visited = set()
        parent = {}
        
        def dfs_cycle(v):
            if v in visited:
                return True
            visited.add(v)
            stack.append(v)
            
            for neighbor in graph[v]:
                if neighbor in stack:
                    # Found cycle
                    return True
                if neighbor not in visited:
                    if dfs_cycle(neighbor):
                        return True
            
            stack.pop()
            return False
        
        return dfs_cycle(start_v)
    
    # Actually, we need to check if there's any reachable cycle
    # Let's check from the start vertex
    
    # Build reverse graph to find all reachable nodes
    reverse_graph = defaultdict(list)
    for u in graph:
        for v in graph[u]:
            reverse_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # BFS to find all reachable nodes
    queue = deque([start])
    reachable = set([start])
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor not in reachable:
                reachable.add(neighbor)
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Check if any reachable node has a cycle
    # We can use cycle detection on the reachable subgraph
    visited = set()
    stack = []
    
    def has_reachable_cycle(v):
        if v in visited:
            return False
            
        visited.add(v)
        stack.append(v)
        
        for next_v in graph[v]:
            if next_v in stack:
                return True
            if next_v not in visited:
                if has_reachable_cycle(next_v):
                    return True
        
        stack.pop()
        return False
    
    cycle_found = False
    for v in reachable:
        if has_reachable_cycle(v):
            cycle_found = True
            break
    
    if cycle_found:
        print("Draw")
        return
    
    # If we reach here, Petya cannot win and cannot draw
    print("Lose")
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge to an adjacent vertex
5. The player who cannot move loses
6. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

This is a classic game theory problem on directed graphs. The key insights are:

1. **Winning condition**: Petya wins if he can force a position where Vasya has no moves (terminal state)
2. **Losing condition**: Petya loses if every possible move leads to a position where Vasya can force a win
3. **Draw condition**: Petya can move in cycles that never end (game lasts > 10^6 turns)

## Strategy

1. **Identify terminal states**: Vertices with no outgoing edges (dead-ends)
2. **Game state analysis**: 
   - A position is winning if there exists at least one move to a losing position for the opponent
   - A position is losing if all moves lead to winning positions for the opponent
3. **Cycle detection**: If there's a cycle in the game, the game can go on forever (draw)

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from input
2. Find all vertices with no outgoing edges (terminal states)
3. Use DFS or dynamic programming to determine winning/losing positions
4. Check if we can reach a terminal state in a finite number of moves
5. Check if there are cycles that can be used to achieve a draw

## Implementation Approach

I'll use a recursive approach with memoization to determine game states, but also detect cycles to handle the draw case.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            vertices = line[1:]
            graph[i] = vertices
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Find all vertices with no outgoing edges (terminal states)
    terminal_vertices = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal_vertices.add(i)
    
    # Check if we can reach a terminal state in a finite number of moves
    # We'll use DFS to find the shortest path to a terminal state
    
    # Check if there's a path from s to any terminal vertex
    visited = set()
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited.add(s)
    
    # We need to find the shortest path to any terminal vertex
    # Also track if we can reach a terminal state
    
    path_to_terminal = None
    
    while queue:
        current, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if current is terminal
        if current in terminal_vertices:
            path_to_terminal = path[:]
            break
            
        # Explore neighbors
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, path + [next_vertex]))
    
    # If we found a path to a terminal state, Petya can win
    if path_to_terminal:
        print("Win")
        print(*path_to_terminal)
        return
    
    # If no path to terminal state, check for cycles
    # We need to detect if there's a cycle that can be traversed infinitely
    
    # Use DFS with state tracking to detect cycles
    # A state is (vertex, path) where path is the current path
    # We want to detect if we can enter a cycle that doesn't end
    
    # First, let's see if we can detect any cycles from s
    visited_states = set()
    path_stack = []
    
    def has_cycle_from_state(start, path):
        if start in terminal_vertices:
            return False
        
        # Check if we've seen this state before
        state = (start, tuple(path))
        if state in visited_states:
            return True  # cycle detected
        
        visited_states.add(state)
        
        # Explore neighbors
        for next_vertex in graph[start]:
            if has_cycle_from_state(next_vertex, path + [next_vertex]):
                return True
        
        return False
    
    # Check if we can detect a cycle that leads to infinite play
    # If we can enter a cycle and stay in it, it's a draw
    
    # Simple approach: if we can visit a vertex more than once in the path, we might have a cycle
    # But we need to ensure it's a non-terminating cycle
    
    # We'll do a BFS to find if we can visit a vertex multiple times
    visited_vertices = set()
    path = []
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    
    while queue:
        current, current_path = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if we've visited this vertex before in current path
        if current in current_path[:-1]:  # already visited in this path
            # We found a cycle! This leads to a draw
            # But we need to make sure it's not terminating
            # Actually, we need to find a cycle that doesn't end
            # If we found any cycle, it's a draw
            
            # We can also check if the cycle leads to non-terminating play
            print("Draw")
            return
        
        # Check if current is terminal (we don't process further)
        if current in terminal_vertices:
            continue
            
        # Otherwise, explore neighbors
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            new_path = current_path + [next_vertex]
            queue.append((next_vertex, new_path))
            
        # We also need to check if we can reach a terminal state from here
        # But we already handled that above
    
    # If we get here, no cycle and no terminal state reachable
    # Check if there's any vertex that can lead to infinite play
    
    # Simpler approach: if there's a path to terminal and we found it, we win
    # Otherwise, if we can detect a cycle (vertex appears twice), it's a draw
    # Otherwise, it's a lose
    
    # Actually, let's implement a more robust cycle detection
    
    # Use iterative DFS with path tracking
    def detect_cycle():
        visited = set()
        rec_stack = set()
        path = []
        
        def dfs(v):
            if v in rec_stack:
                return True  # cycle found
            if v in visited:
                return False
            
            visited.add(v)
            rec_stack.add(v)
            
            if v in graph:
                for neighbor in graph[v]:
                    if dfs(neighbor):
                        return True
            
            rec_stack.remove(v)
            return False
        
        # Check if any vertex in the reachable component has a cycle
        reachable = set()
        stack = [s]
        while stack:
            v = stack.pop()
            if v not in reachable:
                reachable.add(v)
                if v in graph:
                    for neighbor in graph[v]:
                        if neighbor not in reachable:
                            stack.append(neighbor)
        
        # Check cycles in reachable components
        for v in reachable:
            if v not in visited:
                if dfs(v):
                    return True
        return False
    
    # Actually, let me simplify - if there's a path to terminal, we win
    # If there's a cycle that we can enter, we draw
    # Otherwise, we lose
    
    # But we need to check if we can enter a cycle from start
    
    # First, let's check if we can reach any terminal from start
    # We already did that above - if path_to_terminal exists, we win
    
    # If not, check if we can reach a cycle
    
    # Use BFS to detect cycles in reachable components
    visited_bfs = set()
    queue = deque([s])
    visited_bfs.add(s)
    
    # For each vertex, store the path to it (only for cycle detection)
    # We'll track the distance from start and whether a vertex appears multiple times
    
    # Simpler: if we can find a vertex that has a back edge to an earlier vertex in the path, cycle exists
    
    # Do BFS and track paths to detect cycles
    from collections import defaultdict
    
    # Track the path to each vertex
    path_to = {s: []}
    
    queue = deque([s])
    
    while queue:
        current = queue.popleft()
        
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            if next_vertex not in path_to:
                path_to[next_vertex] = path_to[current] + [next_vertex]
                queue.append(next_vertex)
            else:
                # Found a cycle? Actually, we need to check if it forms a cycle
                # But we only store one path, so this doesn't guarantee cycle
                pass
    
    # Check if we can find any vertex that has a cycle
    # For any vertex v, if we reach it via two different paths, we have a cycle
    
    # Actually, let's do this: after BFS, check if any vertex appears in a cycle
    # We'll use the fact that if from vertex u we can go to v and later we can go back to u, it's a cycle
    
    # Alternative approach from known solutions to similar problems:
    # - If we can reach a terminal vertex, Petya wins
    # - If we can reach a cycle (a loop), Petya can draw by looping forever
    # - Otherwise, Petya loses
    
    # Let me implement a proper cycle detection in the reachable component
    
    # Find all vertices reachable from s
    reachable = set()
    queue = deque([s])
    reachable.add(s)
    
    while queue:
        current = queue.popleft()
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            if next_vertex not in reachable:
                reachable.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append(next_vertex)
    
    # Now check if there's any cycle in the reachable component
    visited_cycle = set()
    rec_stack = set()
    
    def has_cycle_dfs(v):
        if v in rec_stack:
            return True
        if v in visited_cycle:
            return False
            
        visited_cycle.add(v)
        rec_stack.add(v)
        
        if v in graph:
            for neighbor in graph[v]:
                if has_cycle_dfs(neighbor):
                    return True
        
        rec_stack.remove(v)
        return False
    
    # Check if any reachable vertex has a cycle
    for v in reachable:
        if v not in visited_cycle:
            if has_cycle_dfs(v):
                print("Draw")
                return
    
    # If we reach here, no cycle and no terminal reachable
    print("Lose")
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            vertices = line[1:]
            graph[i] = vertices
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # First, check if we can reach a terminal vertex (no outgoing edges)
    terminal_vertices = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal_vertices.add(i)
    
    # BFS to find shortest path to any terminal vertex
    visited = set()
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited.add(s)
    path_to_terminal = None
    
    while queue:
        current, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if current is terminal
        if current in terminal_vertices:
            path_to_terminal = path[:]
            break
            
        # Explore neighbors
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, path + [next_vertex]))
    
    # If we found a path to terminal, Petya wins
    if path_to_terminal:
        print("Win")
        print(*path_to_terminal)
        return
    
    # Check for cycles in the reachable component
    # Find all vertices reachable from s
    reachable = set()
    queue = deque([s])
    reachable.add(s)
    
    while queue:
        current = queue.popleft()
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            if next_vertex not in reachable:
                reachable.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append(next_vertex)
    
    # Use DFS to detect cycles in reachable component
    visited_dfs = set()
    rec_stack = set()
    
    def has_cycle(v):
        if v in rec_stack:
            return True
        if v in visited_dfs:
            return False
            
        visited_dfs.add(v)
        rec_stack.add(v)
        
        if v in graph:
            for neighbor in graph[v]:
                if has_cycle(neighbor):
                    return True
        
        rec_stack.remove(v)
        return False
    
    # Check if any reachable vertex has a cycle
    for v in reachable:
        if v not in visited_dfs:
            if has_cycle(v):
                print("Draw")
                return
    
    # If no cycle and no terminal reachable, Petya loses
    print("Lose")
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Petya and Vasya arranged a game. The game runs by the following rules. Players have a directed graph consisting of n vertices and m edges. One of the vertices contains a chip. Initially the chip is located at vertex s. Players take turns moving the chip along some edge of the graph. Petya goes first. Player who can't move the chip loses. If the game lasts for 106 turns the draw is announced.

Vasya was performing big laboratory work in "Spelling and parts of speech" at night before the game, so he fell asleep at the very beginning of the game. Petya decided to take the advantage of this situation and make both Petya's and Vasya's moves.

Your task is to help Petya find out if he can win the game or at least draw a tie.

Input

The first line of input contain two integers n and m — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph (2 ≤ n ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ 2·105).

The next n lines contain the information about edges of the graph. i-th line (1 ≤ i ≤ n) contains nonnegative integer ci — number of vertices such that there is an edge from i to these vertices and ci distinct integers ai, j — indices of these vertices (1 ≤ ai, j ≤ n, ai, j ≠ i).

It is guaranteed that the total sum of ci equals to m.

The next line contains index of vertex s — the initial position of the chip (1 ≤ s ≤ n).

Output

If Petya can win print «Win» in the first line. In the next line print numbers v1, v2, ..., vk (1 ≤ k ≤ 106) — the sequence of vertices Petya should visit for the winning. Vertex v1 should coincide with s. For i = 1... k - 1 there should be an edge from vi to vi + 1 in the graph. There must be no possible move from vertex vk. The sequence should be such that Petya wins the game.

If Petya can't win but can draw a tie, print «Draw» in the only line. Otherwise print «Lose».

Examples

2 4 5
1 4
1 5
0
1


Output

Win
1 2 4 5 


Input

3 2
1 3
1 1
0
2

Lose

1 2

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge to an adjacent vertex
5. The player who cannot move loses
6. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

This is essentially a "game theory" problem on a directed graph. We need to analyze the game states:

1. **Winning state**: A position where the current player can force a win
2. **Losing state**: A position where no matter what the current player does, the opponent can force a win
3. **Drawing state**: A position where the game can go on forever (infinite cycle)

The strategy:
- If we can reach a dead end (no outgoing edges) on an odd-numbered turn (Petya's turn), Petya wins
- If we can reach a dead end on an even-numbered turn (Vasya's turn), Petya loses
- If we can enter an infinite cycle, it's a draw

## Algorithm

1. First, check if there's a path from the starting position to a dead end (no outgoing edges)
2. If such a path exists and reaches a dead end on Petya's turn (odd number of moves), Petya wins
3. If no such path exists but we can enter a cycle, it's a draw
4. Otherwise, Petya loses

We can approach this with:
- **Reachability from start to dead ends** 
- **Cycle detection** (to check for draws)

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Build the graph from input
2. Find all vertices with no outgoing edges (dead ends)
3. Use BFS/DFS to find if there's a path from start to any dead end
4. Check if such path has odd length (Petya wins)
5. Check if there's any cycle reachable from start (draw)
6. Otherwise, Petya loses

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            vertices = line[1:]
            for v in vertices:
                graph[i].append(v)
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Find all vertices with no outgoing edges (dead ends)
    dead_ends = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            dead_ends.add(i)
    
    # Check if we can reach a dead end in an odd number of moves (Petya wins)
    # BFS to find shortest path to dead ends from start
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])  # (current_vertex, path)
    visited = {s}
    found_win = False
    win_path = []
    
    while queue:
        vertex, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # If this is a dead end and it's an odd distance from start (Petya's turn)
        if vertex in dead_ends:
            # Length of path - 1 is the number of moves made
            # If path length is odd, Petya made the last move
            if len(path) % 2 == 1:  # Odd number of vertices means odd number of moves (Petya's turn)
                found_win = True
                win_path = path[:]
                break
        
        # Explore all neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[vertex]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                visited.add(neighbor)
                queue.append((neighbor, path + [neighbor]))
    
    if found_win:
        print("Win")
        print(*win_path)
        return
    
    # Check if we can draw (reach a cycle)
    # Use DFS with path tracking to detect cycle
    visited = set()
    recursion_stack = set()
    path = []
    
    def has_cycle(v, current_path):
        if v in recursion_stack:
            return True
        
        if v in visited:
            return False
            
        visited.add(v)
        recursion_stack.add(v)
        
        # Check all neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if has_cycle(neighbor, current_path + [v]):
                return True
        
        # If we reach here, no cycle found from this path
        recursion_stack.remove(v)
        return False
    
    # Check if there's any cycle reachable from start
    # Simple way: if there's a cycle in any reachable component, we can draw
    # We can just check if we can return to any vertex in the current path
    
    # Alternative: if we can reach a vertex that we've already visited in current path
    def find_cycle():
        visited = set()
        path = []
        
        def dfs(v):
            if v in path:
                # Found cycle
                cycle_start = path.index(v)
                return True
            
            if v in visited:
                return False
            
            visited.add(v)
            path.append(v)
            
            for neighbor in graph[v]:
                if dfs(neighbor):
                    return True
                    
            path.pop()
            return False
        
        # Check if any path from start leads to cycle
        if dfs(s):
            return True
        
        return False
    
    if find_cycle():
        print("Draw")
        return
    
    # If we can't win and can't draw, Petya loses
    print("Lose")

solve()
```
